, and hence it can be semi-regular for at most finitely many values of
d
.

Lemma 8.2.5
.

We have

ch({\mathcal{O}}_{W_{2}})=\Theta^{2}/2-\Theta^{3}/3+3[pt]
in the notation of Example
8.2.3
.

Proof.

We have
ch({\mathcal{O}}_{W_{2}})=\Theta^{2}/2+\lambda\Theta^{3}+3[pt]
, for some rational number
\lambda
satisfying

(8.2.7)

\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{W_{2}}(-\Theta))=\int_{X}\left(\Theta^{2}/2+\lambda\Theta^{3}+3[pt]\right)\left(1-\Theta+\Theta^{2}/2-\Theta^{3}/6+\Theta^{4}/24\right)=9-24\lambda,

since
\dim H^{3,3}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
is the rank of the Neron-Severi group of
X
, which is
1
for generic
C
.
Assume that
C
is the intersection of a smooth quadric
Q
and a cubic in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
. So
C
has two
g^{1}_{3}
’s, associated to the two rulings of
Q
.
Let
q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3}
be a reduced divisor in one
g^{1}_{3}
, denote by
{\mathcal{L}}:=\omega_{C}(-q_{1}-q_{2}-q_{3})
the other
g^{1}_{3}
, and let
p\in C\setminus\{q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\}
be a point, such that
{\mid}{\mathcal{L}}(-p){\mid}=\{a+b\}
for distinct points
a,b\in C\setminus\{p\}
.
We calculate the intersection
W_{2}\cap\tau_{p-q_{1}-q_{2}}(\Theta)
.
The intersection has cohomology class
\Theta^{3}/2
. It consists of the union of three irreducible components of class
\Theta^{3}/6
each.
As a subset of
X={\rm Pic}^{2}(C)
, the intersection consists of classes of effective divisors
D
on
C
of degree
2
, such that
D+q_{1}+q_{2}-p
is effective.
One irreducible component is
C_{1}:=\tau_{p}(AJ(C))
.

The complement of
C_{1}
in the intersection consist of divisors
D
, such that
h^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{C}(D+q_{1}+q_{2})=2
(the inequality
h^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{C}(D+q_{1}+q_{2})\leq 2
follows from the assumption that
C
is not hyperelliptic). Such divisors
D
satisfy

\omega_{C}(-D^{\prime})\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(D+q_{1}+q_{2}),

for some effective divisor
D^{\prime}
. So
D+D^{\prime}
belongs to
{\mid}\omega_{C}(-q_{1}-q_{2}){\mid}
.
If
q_{3}
is in the support of
D
, then
D=q_{3}+q
, where
q
is any point of
C
, since
h^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{C}(q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3}))=2
.
Hence, a second irreducible component is
C_{2}:=\tau_{q_{3}}(AJ(C))
.
The third irreducible component
C_{3}
consists of
D
, such that
D+q\in{\mid}{\mathcal{L}}{\mid}
, for some
q\in C
. So
C_{3}=\tau_{{\mathcal{L}}}(-AJ(C))
,
where
-AJ(C)
is a curve in
{\rm Pic}^{-1}(C)
.

The curves
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
intersect at the point corresponding to the divisor
p+q_{3}
. The curves
C_{1}
and
C_{3}
intersect at the two points

\{p+a,p+b\}
.
Let
P
be the plane tangent to
Q
at
q_{3}
. Then
P\cap Q
consists of two lines through
q_{3}
and
P\cap C=q_{1}+q_{2}+2q_{3}+a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}
, for some points
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
of
C
, which we may assume distinct, possibly by changing the choice of the divisor
q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3}
.
The curves
C_{2}
and
C_{3}
intersect at the two points
\{q_{3}+a^{\prime},q_{3}+b^{\prime}\}
.
We conclude that

\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{W_{2}\cap\tau_{p-q_{1}-q_{2}}(\Theta)})=\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{C_{1}})+\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{C_{2}})+\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{C_{3}})-5=-14.

\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{W_{2}}(-\tau_{p-q_{1}-q_{2}}(\Theta)))=\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{W_{2}})-\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{W_{2}\cap\tau_{p-q_{1}-q_{2}}(\Theta)})=17.

Comparing with (
8.2.7
) we get
9-24\lambda=17
, so
\lambda=-1/3
.
∎

8.3.
Secant sheaves on abelian threefolds with a rank
6
obstruction map

Keep the notation of Lemma
8.2.1
.
Set
F_{1}:={\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{d+1}C_{i}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(\Theta)
. Let
P:={\rm span}\{\alpha,\beta\}
be the rational
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-d})
-secant plane.
Assumption
2.4.1
is satisfied, since
(\alpha,\beta)_{S}=\int_{X}\tau(\alpha)\cup\beta=\int_{X}\alpha\cup\beta=-4d\neq 0.

Let
h
be an ample class in the rank
1
subgroup
H^{2}(X\times\hat{X},{\mathbb{Z}})^{{\rm Spin}(V)_{P}}
. Such an ample class
h
exists, by Proposition
2.4.4
.

Lemma 8.3.1
.

The rank of
\Phi(F_{1}\boxtimes F_{1})
is non-zero.
The
{\rm Spin}(V)_{P}
-invariant classes
h^{3}
and
\kappa_{3}(\Phi(F_{1}\boxtimes F_{1}))
are linearly independent.

Proof.

We use the notation of Proposition
6.4.1
.
We have

ch(\Phi(F_{1}\boxtimes F_{1}))=\phi(ch(F_{1})\boxtimes ch(F_{1}))=\phi^{\prime}(ch(F_{1})\boxtimes\tau(ch(F_{1}))).

Set
\lambda_{1}:=\exp(\sqrt{-d}\Theta)
and
\lambda_{2}=\bar{\lambda}_{1}
, so that

ch(F_{1})=\frac{1}{2}\left[(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})+\frac{1}{\sqrt{-d}}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\right]=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{-d}}\left[(1+\sqrt{-d})\lambda_{1}+(-1+\sqrt{-d})\lambda_{2}\right]

Now,
\tau
interchanges
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
. Hence,

\displaystyle ch(F_{1})\!\boxtimes\!\tau(ch(F_{1}))\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\frac{-1}{4d}\!\left[[(1\!\!+\!\!\sqrt{-d})\lambda_{1}\!+\!(-1\!\!+\!\!\sqrt{-d})\lambda_{2}]\boxtimes[(-1\!\!+\!\!\sqrt{-d})\lambda_{1}\!+\!(1\!\!+\!\!\sqrt{-d})\lambda_{2}]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\frac{d\!+\!1}{4d}[\lambda_{1}\!\!\boxtimes\!\!\lambda_{1}\!+\!\lambda_{2}\!\!\boxtimes\!\!\lambda_{2}]\!+\!\frac{d\!-\!1}{4d}[\lambda_{1}\!\!\boxtimes\!\!\lambda_{2}\!+\!\lambda_{2}\!\!\boxtimes\!\!\lambda_{1}]\!+\!\frac{\sqrt{-d}}{2d}[\lambda_{2}\!\!\boxtimes\!\!\lambda_{1}\!-\!\lambda_{1}\!\!\boxtimes\!\!\lambda_{2}]

The rank of
\Phi(F_{1}\boxtimes F_{1})
is non-zero, by Proposition
6.4.1
(
2
),
since the coefficient of
[\lambda_{2}\!\!\boxtimes\!\!\lambda_{1}\!-\!\lambda_{1}\!\!\boxtimes\!\!\lambda_{2}]
is non-zero.
We prove that the classes

\kappa(\Phi(F_{1}\boxtimes F_{1}))
and
h^{3}
are linearly independent, by contradiction. Assume otherwise. Then
\kappa(\Phi(F_{1}\boxtimes F_{1}))

belongs to the subring generated by powers of
h
and is thus
{\rm Spin}(V_{K})_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
-
\rho
-invariant, by Lemma
2.2.7
. Hence,
ch(\Phi(F_{1}\boxtimes F_{1}))
is
{\rm Spin}(V_{K})_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
-
\rho^{\prime}
-invariant, by Lemma
6.2.6
.
It follows that
ch(F_{1})\boxtimes\tau(ch(F_{1}))
is invariant under
{\rm Spin}(V_{K})_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
, by the
{\rm Spin}(V)
-
\rho^{\prime}
-equivariance of
\phi\circ(id\otimes\tau).

But the last two summands

\lambda_{1}\!\!\boxtimes\!\!\lambda_{2}\!+\!\lambda_{2}\!\!\boxtimes\!\!\lambda_{1}
and

\lambda_{2}\!\!\boxtimes\!\!\lambda_{1}\!-\!\lambda_{1}\!\!\boxtimes\!\!\lambda_{2}

in the displayed formula above are
{\rm Spin}(V_{K})_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
-invariant, while the first summand is a scalar multiple of
\lambda_{1}\!\!\boxtimes\!\!\lambda_{1}\!+\!\lambda_{2}\!\!\boxtimes\!\!\lambda_{2}
and is a not
{\rm Spin}(V_{K})_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
-invariant, by Lemma
2.2.7
and Proposition
6.4.1
(
1
).
Hence,
ch(F_{1})\boxtimes\tau(ch(F_{1}))
is not
{\rm Spin}(V_{K})_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
-invariant,
since the coefficient of
\lambda_{1}\!\!\boxtimes\!\!\lambda_{1}\!+\!\lambda_{2}\!\!\boxtimes\!\!\lambda_{2}
is non zero. A contradiction.
∎

Set
F:={\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{n}C_{i}}
.
Let
U\subset({\rm Pic}^{0}(C))^{n}
be the Zariski open subset of points
(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})
, such that translating each
C_{i}
by
\ell_{i}
results in
n
disjoint curves. We have a natural morphism from
U
to the Hilbert scheme of
X
.
Explicitly, let
Z_{i}
be the product
X^{i-1}\times C_{i}\times X^{n-i}
, for
1\leq i\leq n
. Then
F
is the pullback of the ideal of the union
\cup_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}
via trhe diagonal embedding of
X
in
X^{n}
.

{\rm Pic}^{0}(C)^{n}
acts on
X^{n}
introducing the desired map from the set
U
to the Hilbert scheme of
X
.
The symmetric group
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
acts freely on
U
as follows. If
(t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{n})\in{\rm Pic}^{1}(C)^{n}
translates
C\times\cdots\times C
to
C_{1}\times C_{2}\times\cdots\times C_{n}
, then
U
is invariant with respect to the action on
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)^{n}
which is conjugated by translation by
(t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{n})
to the permutation action on
{\rm Pic}^{1}(X)^{n}
and the action on
U
is fixed point free.
The connected component of
F
in the moduli space of simple sheaves on
X
contains a smooth subscheme isomorphic to
(U/{\mathfrak{S}}_{n})\times{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
obtained by translating the
C_{i}
’s and tensoring
F
by line bundles.
Hence, we have a canonical injective homomorphism

(8.3.1)

H^{0}(X,TX)^{n}\oplus H^{1}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{1}(F,F)

and

\dim{\rm Ext}^{1}(F,F)\geq 3n+3
.

Lemma 8.3.2
.

The homomorphism (
8.3.1
) is an isomorphism. Consequently,
\dim Ext^{1}(F,F)=3n+3
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the inequality
\dim{\rm Ext}^{1}(F,F)\leq 3n+3
.
Set
{\mathcal{I}}_{k}:={\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{k}C_{i}}
. We have the short exact sequence

(8.3.2)

0\rightarrow{\mathcal{I}}_{n}\rightarrow{\mathcal{I}}_{n-1}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n}}\rightarrow 0

and the long exact

0\rightarrow{\rm Hom}({\mathcal{I}}_{n},{\mathcal{I}}_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}{\rm Hom}({\mathcal{I}}_{n},{\mathcal{I}}_{n-1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0}}{{\rightarrow}}{\rm Hom}({\mathcal{I}}_{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n}})\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{n},{\mathcal{I}}_{n})\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{n},{\mathcal{I}}_{n-1})

We have the isomorphism
{\rm Hom}({\mathcal{I}}_{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n}})\cong H^{0}(C_{n},N_{C_{n}/X})
.
The quotient of
H^{0}(C_{n},N_{C_{n}/X})
by
H^{0}(C_{n},TX_{\mid_{C_{n}}})
is the kernel of the differential of the Torelli map

H^{1}(C_{n},TC_{n})\rightarrow H^{1}(C_{n},TX_{\mid_{C_{n}}})\cong H^{0,1}(C_{n})\otimes H^{0,1}(C_{n})
, and the latter is injective for our non-hyperelliptic curve
C_{n}
, by Noether’s theorem. Hence,
H^{0}(C_{n},N_{C_{n}/X})
is
3
-dimensional. It remains to prove the inequality

\dim{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{n},{\mathcal{I}}_{n-1})\leq 3n
. We will prove by induction on
n
the equality

\dim{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{n},{\mathcal{I}}_{n-1})=3n
.

When
n=1
,
{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{n},{\mathcal{I}}_{n-1})\cong H^{2}({\mathcal{I}}_{1})^{*}\cong H^{2}(X,{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{1}})^{*}
is
3
-dimensional. Indeed, we have more generally the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\frac{\oplus_{i=1}^{n}H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C_{i}})}{H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})}\rightarrow H^{2}({\mathcal{I}}_{n})\rightarrow H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\rightarrow 0,

obtained from the long exact sequence of cohomology associated to the short exact
0\rightarrow{\mathcal{I}}_{n}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\rightarrow\oplus_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{i}}\rightarrow 0

using the injectivity of
H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\rightarrow\oplus_{i=1}^{n}H^{1}(C_{i},{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{i}})
.

Assume that
n\geq 2
and
\dim{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{I}}_{n-2})=3(n-1)
. Then
\dim{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{I}}_{n-1})=3n
, as shown above.
Consider long exact sequence obtained from the short exact sequence (
8.3.2
)

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{I}}_{n-1})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}{\rm Hom}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0}}{{\rightarrow}}{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{I}}_{n})\rightarrow

\displaystyle{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{I}}_{n-1})

\displaystyle\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\xi}}{{\rightarrow}}{\rm Ext}^{2}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{I}}_{n})\rightarrow

\displaystyle{\rm Ext}^{2}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{I}}_{n-1})

\displaystyle\rightarrow 0.

The sheaves
{\mathcal{E}}xt^{i}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n}})
vanish, for
i>0
. Hence, the local to global spectral sequence computing
{\rm Ext}^{i}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n}})
degenerates at the
E_{2}
term. We get the
vanishing of
{\rm Ext}^{i}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n}})
, for
i>1
and the
isomorphism

{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n}})\cong H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n}})
and the latter is
3
-dimensional.
Furthermore, the composition

H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\cong H^{1}({\mathcal{E}}nd({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{I}}_{n-1}))\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{I}}_{n-1})\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n}})\cong H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n}})

is surjective. Hence, the connecting homomorphism
\xi
vanishes and

\dim{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{n},{\mathcal{I}}_{n-1})=\dim{\rm Ext}^{2}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{I}}_{n})=\dim{\rm Ext}^{2}({\mathcal{I}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{I}}_{n-1})=3n,

where the first equality is by Serre’s duality, the second by the vanishing of
\xi
, and the third was established above via the induction hypothesis.
∎

Let
\Delta_{X}\subset X\times X
be the diagonal.
A class in the Hochschild cohomology
HH^{i}(X):={\rm Hom}({\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta_{X}},{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta_{X}}[i])
corresponds to a natural transformation from the identity endofunctor of
D^{b}(X)
to its shift by
i
.
The evaluation homomorphism

(8.3.3)

ev_{F}:HH^{*}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{*}(F,F)

is a graded algebra homomorphism. Denote by
ev_{F}^{i}:HH^{i}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{i}(F,F)
the restriction of
ev_{F}
to
HH^{i}(X)
.

The second Hochschild cohomology
HH^{2}(X)
parametrizes first order deformations of
D^{b}(X)
.
Let

ob_{F}:HH^{2}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{2}(F,F)

be the homomorphism
ev_{F}^{2}
.
The kernel of
ob_{F}
parametrizes those deformations along which
F
deforms to first order, by
[
T
]
. As above, we set
F:={\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{n}C_{i}}
, where
n\geq 1
. Denote by
ob_{F}:HT^{2}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{2}(F,F)
also the composition of
ob_{F}
above with the HKR isomorphism
HT^{2}(X):=H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\oplus H^{1}(TX)\oplus H^{0}(\wedge^{2}TX)\cong HH^{2}(X)
. Then
ob_{F}
is given by contraction with the exponential Atiyah class
\exp(at_{E})
, by
[
Hua
, Th. A]
.

Lemma 8.3.3
.

{\rm rank}(ob_{F})\geq 6.

Proof.

Consider the contraction homomorphism

H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\oplus H^{1}(TX)\oplus H^{0}(\wedge^{2}TX)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rfloor ch(F)}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\oplus H^{3}(\Omega^{1}_{X}).

It restricts to the first summand as an isomorphism onto the first summand of the co-domain, as
ch_{0}(F)=1
, and to
the second summand as an isomorphism onto the second summand of the co-domain, as
ch_{2}(F)=\frac{-n}{2}\Theta^{2}
.
Hence, the above homomorphism is sujective and so its kernel has co-dimension
6
. The kernel of
ob_{F}
is contained in the kernel of the above homomorphism, by
[
Hua
, Theorem B]
, hence
ob_{F}
has rank
\geq 6
.
∎

Diagram (
7.1.2
) extends to the commutative diagram

(8.3.4)

\textstyle{HT^{2}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ob_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\rfloor ch(E)}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}^{2}(E,E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{\prod_{q=0}^{d-2}H^{q+2}(M,\Omega^{q}_{M})}

by
[
BF2
, Prop. 6.2.1 and Cor. 6.3.2]
.

Lemma 8.3.4
.

If the kernels of
ob_{E}
and
\rfloor ch(E)
in
HT^{2}(M)
are equal and
ob_{E}
is surjective, then
E
is semiregular.

Proof.

The hypotheses imply that there exists a unique injective map
\sigma^{\prime}:{\rm Ext}^{2}(E,E)\rightarrow\prod_{q=0}^{d-2}H^{q+2}(M,\Omega^{q}_{M})
, such that

\rfloor ch(E)=\sigma^{\prime}\circ ob_{E}.

The equality
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
follows from the commutativity of Diagram (
8.3.4
).
∎

Remark 8.3.5
.

If we drop the assumption that
ob_{E}
is surjective in Lemma
8.3.4
we still conclude that the semiregularity map restricts to the image of the obstruction map as an injective map.

Remark 8.3.6
.

Note that the hypotheses of Lemma
8.3.4

are invariant under derived equivalences. If
\Phi:D^{b}(M)\rightarrow D^{b}(M^{\prime})
is an equivalence of derived categories,
E
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
8.3.4
, and
\Phi(E)
is represented by a coherent sheaf
E^{\prime}
, then
E^{\prime}
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
8.3.4
and is thus semiregular as well.
The space
\prod_{q=0}^{d-2}H^{q+2}(M,\Omega^{q}_{M})
in the above diagrams is the graded summand
H\Omega_{-2}(M)
of the Hochschild homology
HH_{*}(M)
.
The equivalence
\Phi
induces isomorphisms
\Phi:{\rm Ext}^{2}(E,E)\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{2}(E^{\prime},E^{\prime})
and

\Phi_{*}:H\Omega_{-2}(M)\rightarrow H\Omega_{-2}(M^{\prime})
. The hypotheses of Lemma
8.3.4
imply
that the semiregularity map
\sigma^{\prime}
of
E^{\prime}
is the conjugate of the semiregularity map
\sigma
of
E
,

\sigma^{\prime}\circ\Phi=\Phi_{*}\circ\sigma
. It is natural to expect that the latter equality holds, more generally, without the hypotheses of Lemma
8.3.4
.

Lemma 8.3.7
.

(1)

The algebra
{\rm Ext}^{*}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})
is generated by
{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})
.

(2)

The homomorphism
ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}:HT^{*}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{*}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})
is surjective and its kernel is the annihilator
{\rm ann}(ch({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}))
of the Chern character
1-\frac{1}{2}\Theta^{2}+2[pt]
of
{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}
.

0\rightarrow{\rm ann}(ch({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}))\cap HT^{2}(X)\rightarrow\begin{array}[]{c}H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\\
\oplus\\
H^{1}(TX)\\
\oplus\\
H^{0}(\wedge^{2}TX)\end{array}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&-\Theta^{2}/2\\
0&-\Theta^{2}/2&2[pt]\end{array}\right)}}{{\longrightarrow}}\begin{array}[]{c}H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\\
\oplus\\
H^{3}(\Omega^{1}_{X})\end{array}\rightarrow 0.

(3)

The sheaf
{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}
is semiregular.

Proof.

(
1
)
This is the case
n=1
. In this case
{\rm Ext}^{2}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})
is
6
-dimensional,
by Lemma
8.3.2
, and so
ob_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}
is surjective,
by Lemma
8.3.3
. Now
HT^{*}(X)
is generated by
HT^{1}(X)
and
ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}

is an algebra homomorphism. Hence, the surjectivity of
ev^{2}_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}=ob_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}
implies that
the Yoneda product
{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})\otimes{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{2}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})

is surjective. The surjectivity of
{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})\otimes{\rm Ext}^{2}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{3}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})
follows from Serre’s duality.

(
2
)
The homomorphism
ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}^{1}:HT^{1}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})
is an isomorphism, by Lemma

8.3.2
. Hence, the homomorphism
ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}
is surjective,
by part (
1
). The inclusion

\ker(ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}})\subset{\rm ann}(ch({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}))

follows from
[
Hua
, Theorem B]
. Both ideals are graded (homogeneous). Indeed, the homomorphism
ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}
is graded, by definition, and contraction with
ch({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})
maps
HT^{k}(X)
to
\oplus_{q-p=k}H^{p,q}(X)
.
The graded summands of
{\rm ann}(ch({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}))
in
HT^{0}(X)
and
HT^{1}(X)
vanish.
The equality of
\ker(ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}^{2})
and the graded summand of
{\rm ann}(ch({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}))
in
HT^{2}(X)
follows from the proof of Lemma
8.3.3
. The graded summands of both ideals in
HT^{3}(X)
have co-dimension
1
, since
{\rm Ext}^{3}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})
and
H^{3}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
are both one-dimensional, and the summand
H^{3}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
of
HT^{3}(X)
surjects onto both.
Hence the inclusion
\ker(ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}})\subset{\rm ann}(ch({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}))

implies the equality of the graded summands in
HT^{3}(X)
of both ideals.
HT^{k}(X)
is contained in both ideals for
k>3
for degree reasons.

(
3
)
Follows immediately from Part (
2
)
and Lemma
8.3.4
.

∎

The proof of Lemma
8.3.2
identifies the
i
-th direct summand in the domain of (
8.3.1
)
with
H^{0}(C_{i},N_{C_{i}/X})
,
1\leq i\leq n
. Denote this direct summand by
\tilde{E}^{1}_{i}
and set
\tilde{E}_{0}^{1}:=H^{1}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
, so that the domain of (
8.3.1
)
is
\oplus_{i=0}^{n}\tilde{E}^{1}_{i}
.
Note that each
\tilde{E}^{1}_{i}
is
3
-dimensional.
We denote by
E^{1}_{i}
the image of
\tilde{E}^{1}_{i}
via the isomorphism (
8.3.1
).
The Yoneda product
{\rm Ext}^{1}(F,F)\otimes{\rm Ext}^{2}(F,F)\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{3}(F,F)
is a perfect pairing.
Let
E^{2}_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, be the subspace of
{\rm Ext}^{2}(F,F)

annihilating
E^{1}_{0}\oplus\oplus_{j=1,j\neq i}^{n}E^{1}_{i}
. Let
E^{2}_{0}
be the image of
H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
in
{\rm Ext}^{2}(F,F)
.
Then
E^{2}_{i}
is
3
-dimensional, for
0\leq i\leq n
. The Yoneda product restricts to
E^{1}_{0}\otimes E^{2}_{0}\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{3}(F,F)
as a perfect pairing. Indeed, the algebra homomorphism

{\rm Ext}^{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X},{\mathcal{O}}_{X})\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{*}(F,F)

is injective, since it composes with the trace linear homomorphism

tr:{\rm Ext}^{*}(F,F)\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X},{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
to the identity of
{\rm Ext}^{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X},{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
, by
[
HL
, 10.1.3]
and the equality
{\rm rank}(F)=1
.
We get the direct sum decomposition

{\rm Ext}^{2}(F,F)=\oplus_{i=0}^{n}E^{2}_{i}.

When considering different ideal sheaves
{\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{n}C_{i}}
we will denote
E^{i}_{j}
by
E^{i}_{j}({\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{n}C_{i}})
.
We have the isomorphism
F:={\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{n}C_{i}}\cong\otimes_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{i}}
, hence the functor of tensoring with
\otimes_{i=1,i\neq j}^{n}{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{i}}
induces an algebra homomorphism

e_{j}:{\rm Ext}^{*}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{*}(F,F).

The ring structure of the Yoneda algebra
{\rm Ext}^{*}(F,F)
is determined by the following Lemma and Lemma
8.3.7
.

Lemma 8.3.8
.

(1)

The Yoneda product maps
E^{1}_{i}\otimes E^{1}_{j}
to zero in
{\rm Ext}^{2}(F,F)
, if
i\neq j
and
1\leq i,j\leq n
.

(2)

The Yoneda product
{\rm Ext}^{1}(F,F)\otimes{\rm Ext}^{1}(F,F)\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{2}(F,F)
is anti-symmetric.

(3)

The Yoneda product maps
(E^{1}_{0}\oplus E^{1}_{i})\otimes(E^{1}_{0}\oplus E^{1}_{i})
surjectively onto
E^{2}_{0}\oplus E^{2}_{i}
, for all
1\leq i\leq n
. In particular, the algebra
{\rm Ext}^{*}(F,F)
is generated by
{\rm Ext}^{1}(F,F)
.

(4)

The image of
e_{j}
is
{\rm Hom}(F,F)\oplus(E^{1}_{0}\oplus E^{1}_{j})\oplus(E^{2}_{0}\oplus E^{2}_{j})\oplus{\rm Ext}^{3}(F,F).

(5)

The homomorphism
e_{j}
maps
E^{d}_{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})
isomorphically onto
E^{d}_{j}(F)
, for
d=1,2
.

Proof.

(
1
)
Let
\xi_{i}
be a section of
H^{0}(N_{C_{i}/X})
and
\tilde{\xi}_{i}
the corresponding class in
E^{1}_{i}
.
Set
R:={\mathbb{C}}[\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}]/\langle\epsilon_{1}^{2},\epsilon_{2}^{2},\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}\rangle
.
The product
\tilde{\xi}_{i}\circ\tilde{\xi}_{j}
vanishes, if and only if there exists a deformation of
{\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{k=1}^{n}C_{k}}

by an ideal over
X\times{\rm Spec}(R)
, which restricts to
the first order deformation along
\xi_{i}
over
{\rm Spec}({\mathbb{C}}[\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}]/\langle\epsilon_{1}^{2},\epsilon_{2},\rangle)
and to the first order deformation along
\xi_{j}
over

{\rm Spec}({\mathbb{C}}[\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}]/\langle\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}^{2}\rangle)
,
by
[
Ar
, Sec. 2]
.
Assume that
i\neq j
.
Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be the ideal sheaf over
X\times{\rm Spec}(R)
consisting of elements locally of the form

f_{0}+f_{1}\epsilon_{1}+f_{2}\epsilon_{2}
, where

f_{0}\in F:={\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{k=1}^{n}C_{k}},

f_{1}
belongs to
{\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{k=1,k\neq i}^{n}C_{k}}
,

f_{2}
belongs to
{\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{k=1,k\neq j}^{n}C_{k}}
,

(f_{1})_{\mid_{C_{i}}}+df_{0}(\xi_{i})=0,\ \mbox{and}\ (f_{2})_{\mid_{C_{j}}}+df_{0}(\xi_{j})=0.

One easily checks that
{\mathcal{F}}
is indeed an ideal and
{\mathcal{F}}
clearly restricts to the ideals of the two desired first order deformations.

(
2
) The anti-symmetry would follow from that of
(E^{1}_{0}\oplus E^{1}_{i})\otimes(E^{1}_{0}\oplus E^{1}_{i})\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{2}(F,F)
, by
Part (
1
). Now,
E^{1}_{0}\oplus E^{1}_{i}
is equal to
e_{i}({\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{i}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{i}}))
,
e_{i}
is an algebra homomorphism, and
{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{i}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{i}})\otimes{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{i}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{i}})\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{2}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{i}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{i}})
is anti-symmetric,
by the anti-symmetry of the product
HT^{1}(X)\otimes HT^{1}(X)\rightarrow HT^{2}(X)
and the surjectivity of
ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{i}}}
in Lemma
8.3.7
.

(
3
)
We prove first that the image of
(E^{1}_{0}\oplus E^{1}_{i})\otimes(E^{1}_{0}\oplus E^{1}_{i})
is contained in
E^{2}_{0}\oplus E^{2}_{i}
. It suffices to prove that the image of
E^{1}_{i}\otimes{\rm Ext}^{1}(F,F)
is contained in
E^{2}_{0}\oplus E^{2}_{i}
. This follows from part
(
1
) of the lemma as
E^{1}_{i}\otimes{\rm Ext}^{1}(F,F)
annihilates
E^{1}_{j}
, for all
j\neq i
(here we use also the anti-symmetry property in part (
2
)).
Surjectivity would follow from the proof of part (
4
) and
Lemma
8.3.7
.

(
4
) We know that
e_{j}
maps
{\rm Ext}^{k}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})
onto
{\rm Ext}^{k}(F,F)
, for
k=0
and
k=3
. We also know that
e_{j}({\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}))=E^{1}_{0}\oplus E^{1}_{j}.

It remains to show that
e_{j}({\rm Ext}^{2}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}))=E^{2}_{0}\oplus E^{2}_{j}.
Clearly,
e_{j}({\rm Ext}^{2}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}))

is contained in the image of
(E^{1}_{0}\oplus E^{1}_{j})\otimes(E^{1}_{0}\oplus E^{1}_{j})
as
e_{j}
is an algebra homomorphism. Hence,

e_{j}({\rm Ext}^{2}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}))
is contained in
E^{2}_{0}\oplus E^{2}_{j},
by part (
3
). Now the fact that the restriction of
e_{j}
is injective on
{\rm Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})
implies that it is also injective on
{\rm Ext}^{2}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})
,
since the pairing induced by the Yoneda product of their images in
{\rm Ext}^{*}(F,F)
pulls back to the perfect pairing of the Yoneda product in
{\rm Ext}^{*}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})
, as
e_{j}
induces an isomorphism of
{\rm Ext}^{3}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}})
with

{\rm Ext}^{3}(F,F)
. The equality
e_{j}({\rm Ext}^{2}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}},{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}))=E^{2}_{0}\oplus E^{2}_{j}
follows for dimension reasons.

(
5
) The statement is clear for
d=1
. For
d=2
it follows from part (
4
) and the fact that
e_{j}
is an
H^{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
-algebra homomorphism.
∎

Proposition 8.3.9
.

{\rm rank}(ob_{F})=6.

Proof.

We already know that
{\rm rank}(ob_{F})\geq 6
, by Lemma
8.3.3
.
It remains to prove that
{\rm rank}(ob_{F})\leq 6
.
If
n=1
, then
\dim{\rm Ext}^{2}(F,F)=6
, by Lemma
8.3.2
, and so
{\rm rank}(ob_{F})=6
.

Denote by
ev_{F}:HT^{*}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{*}(F,F)
also the composition of (
8.3.3
)
with the HKR isomorphism
HT^{*}(X)\cong HH^{*}(X)
. The HKR isomorphism is an
H^{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
-algebra isomorphism, since the Todd class of
X
vanishes
[
CBR
]
. Hence, the latter
ev_{F}
is an
H^{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
-algebra homomorphism.
An element
\xi\in HT^{1}(X)
decomposes uniquely as the sum
\xi^{\prime}+\xi^{\prime\prime}
with
\xi^{\prime}\in H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
and
\xi^{\prime\prime}\in H^{0}(TX).

We have the following equalities:

(8.3.5)

\displaystyle ev_{F}(\xi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{j}(ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}(\xi^{\prime})),

\displaystyle ev_{F}(\xi^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}e_{j}(ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}(\xi^{\prime\prime})),

by Lemma
8.3.2
.
Note that
e_{j}\circ ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}
is a composition of
H^{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
-algebra homomorphisms.
If
j\neq k
, then
e_{j}(ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}(\xi^{\prime\prime}_{1}))e_{k}(ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{k}}}(\xi_{2}^{\prime\prime}))=0
, for every two elements
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in HT^{1}(X)
, by Lemma
8.3.8
. We get

\displaystyle ev_{F}(\xi_{1}\xi_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[ev_{F}(\xi^{\prime}_{1})+\sum_{j=1}^{n}e_{j}(ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}(\xi^{\prime\prime}_{1}))\right]\left[ev_{F}(\xi^{\prime}_{2})+\sum_{j=1}^{n}e_{j}(ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}(\xi^{\prime\prime}_{2}))\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle ev_{F}(\xi^{\prime}_{1}\xi^{\prime}_{2})+\sum_{j=1}^{n}e_{j}(ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}(\xi^{\prime}_{1}\xi^{\prime\prime}_{2}+\xi^{\prime\prime}_{1}\xi^{\prime}_{2}+\xi^{\prime\prime}_{1}\xi^{\prime\prime}_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}e_{j}\left(ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}\left(\frac{1}{n}\xi^{\prime}_{1}\xi^{\prime}_{2}+\xi^{\prime}_{1}\xi^{\prime\prime}_{2}+\xi^{\prime\prime}_{1}\xi^{\prime}_{2}+\xi^{\prime\prime}_{1}\xi^{\prime\prime}_{2}\right)\right).

The algebra
HT^{*}(X)
is generated by
HT^{1}(X)
. The element
\xi^{\prime}_{1}\xi^{\prime}_{2}
belongs to
H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
,
while the element
\xi^{\prime}_{1}\xi^{\prime\prime}_{2}+\xi^{\prime\prime}_{1}\xi^{\prime}_{2}+\xi^{\prime\prime}_{1}\xi^{\prime\prime}_{2}
belongs to
H^{1}(TX)\oplus H^{0}(\wedge^{2}TX)
.
Thus, the two equations (
8.3.5
) hold also for
\xi\in HT^{2}(X)
under the decomposition
\xi=\xi^{\prime}+\xi^{\prime\prime}
, with
\xi^{\prime}\in H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
and
\xi^{\prime\prime}\in H^{1}(TX)\oplus H^{0}(\wedge^{2}TX)
.

Let
\tau_{ij}:D^{b}(X)\rightarrow D^{b}(X)
be the autoequivalence induced by the translation automorphism mapping
C_{j}
to
C_{i}
. This autoequivalence
acts trivially on
HT^{*}(X)
and
ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{i}}}=\tau_{ij}\circ ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}
. Furthermore,
e_{j}
is injective, by Lemma
8.3.8
.
Hence, the kernel of the composition
e_{j}\circ ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}:HT^{*}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{*}(F,F)

is independent of
j
.
Let

\gamma_{n}:HT^{2}(X)\rightarrow HT^{2}(X)

be the automorphism multiplying the direct summand
H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
by
n
and acting as the identity on

H^{1}(TX)\oplus H^{0}(\wedge^{2}TX)
. We see that
\gamma_{n}
maps the kernel of
e_{j}\circ ob_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}

into that of
ob_{F}
. Now
e_{j}
is injective, by Lemma
8.3.8
, and
ev_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}^{2}=ob_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}}
has rank
6
, by the case
n=1
. Hence,
{\rm rank}(ob_{F})\leq{\rm rank}(ob_{{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{j}}})=6
.

∎

Lemma 8.3.10
.

Let
\Phi:D^{b}(A)\rightarrow D^{b}(B)
be an equivalence of derived categories of two abelian varieties and
F
an object of
D^{b}(A)
. Assume that the kernel of
ob_{F}:HT^{2}(A)\rightarrow{\rm Hom}(F,F[2])
is equal to the subspace annihilating
ch(F)
.
Then the kernel of
ob_{\Phi(F)}
is equal to the subspace annihilating
ch(\Phi(F))
.

Proof.

We have the commutative diagram

\textstyle{H^{*}(A,{\mathbb{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{H}}
\textstyle{HT^{2}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{HT}}
\scriptstyle{ch(F)}
\scriptstyle{\exp(at_{F})}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}(F,F[2])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{H^{*}(B,{\mathbb{C}})}
\textstyle{HT^{2}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ch(E)}
\scriptstyle{\exp(at_{E})}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}(E,E[2]),}

the left square
by
[
CBR
]
, and the right square by
[
Hua
, Theorem A]
.
The vertical arrows are isomorphisms and the kernels of the two horizontal arrows in the top row are equal.
Hence, the same is true for the bottom row.
∎

Remark 8.3.11
.

The above Lemma holds for more general projective varieties, replacing
ch(F)
by the Mukai vector
v(F):=ch(F)td(X)^{\frac{1}{2}}
and factoring the action of
HT^{*}(X)
on its module
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
by the Duflo operator
D:HT^{*}(X)\rightarrow HT^{*}(X)
, given by
D(\alpha)=td(X)^{\frac{1}{2}}\rfloor\alpha
. For abelian varieties
td(X)^{\frac{1}{2}}=1.

Corollary 8.3.12
.

Set
F:={\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{n}C_{i}}(\Theta).

The kernel of
ob_{F}
is equal to the kernel of the homomorphism
(\bullet)\rfloor ch(F):HT^{2}(X)\rightarrow H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
of contraction with the Chern character of
F
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the statement for
F^{\prime}:={\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{n}C_{i}}
, by Lemma
8.3.10
, as
F
is the image of
F^{\prime}
by the autoequivalence of tensorization by
\Theta
. Now,
ch({\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{n}C_{i}})=1-\frac{n}{2}\Theta^{2}+2n[pt]
. The kernel of
ob_{F^{\prime}}
is contained in the kernel
K
of
\rfloor ch(F^{\prime})
, by
[
Hua
, Theorem B]
.
The kernel of
ob_{F^{\prime}}
is
9
dimensional, by Proposition
8.3.9
.
It suffices to show that
\dim(K)\leq 9
. Assume that
\dim(K)>9
. Then the intersection

K\cap[H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\oplus H^{0}(\wedge^{2}TX)]
would be non-trivial.
We claim that
K\cap[H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\oplus H^{0}(\wedge^{2}TX)]=(0).

Indeed, contraction with
ch(F^{\prime})
induces the homomorphism

H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\oplus H^{0}(\wedge^{2}TX)\rightarrow H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\oplus H^{3}(\Omega^{1}_{X})

with upper triangular matrix
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&-\frac{n}{2}\Theta^{2}\\
0&2n[pt]\end{array}\right),
which is invertible
∎

8.4.
Secant
⊠2
-sheaves over
X\times\hat{X}
with
a
9
-dimensional space of unobstructed commutative-gerby deformations

Let
\pi_{i}
,
i=1,2
, be the projections from
X\times X
to
X
. Denote by
at_{F}
the Atiyah class of
F
.
The Atiyah class
at_{\pi_{1}^{*}F}\in{\rm Ext}^{1}(\pi_{1}^{*}F,(\pi_{1}^{*}F)\otimes\Omega^{1}_{X\times X})
of
\pi_{1}^{*}F
is equal to the pushforward of
\pi_{1}^{*}at_{F}\in{\rm Ext}^{1}(\pi_{1}^{*}F,\pi_{1}^{*}(F\otimes\Omega^{1}_{X}))
via the inclusion of
\pi_{1}^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X}
as a direct summand in
\Omega^{1}_{X\times X}

[
BF1
, Prop. 3.14]
. Let
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
be the sheaves in Theorem
1.4.1
.
The Atiyah class of
\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2}
satisfied

(8.4.1)

at_{\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2}}=at_{\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}}\otimes 1+1\otimes at_{\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2}}.

The Künneth decomposition of
{\rm Ext}^{2}(\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2},\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2})
is the direct sum

[{\rm Ext}^{2}(F_{1},F_{1})\otimes{\rm Ext}^{0}(F_{2},F_{2})]\oplus[{\rm Ext}^{0}(F_{1},F_{1})\otimes{\rm Ext}^{2}(F_{2},F_{2})]\oplus[{\rm Ext}^{1}(F_{1},F_{1})\otimes{\rm Ext}^{1}(F_{2},F_{2})].

We have the direct sum decomposition of
HT^{2}(X\times X)

(8.4.2)

\displaystyle HT^{2}(X\times X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{*}HT^{2}(X)\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}HT^{0}(X)\oplus

\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{*}HT^{0}(X)\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}HT^{2}(X)\oplus

\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{*}HT^{1}(X)\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}HT^{1}(X)

Lemma
8.3.2
implies that
\exp(at_{F}):H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\oplus H^{0}(TX)\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{1}(F,F)
is injective.
The obstruction map
ob_{F}:HT^{2}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{2}(F,F)
is the restriction to
HT^{2}(X)
of the algebra homomorphism

(\bullet)\rfloor\exp(at_{F}):HT^{*}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{*}(F,F)

(see
[
Hua
, Theorem A]
).
We see that the obstruction map
ob_{\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2}}
maps the summand in the
i
-th row above into the
i
-th summand of
{\rm Ext}^{2}(\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2},\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2})
in the decomposition displayed above and

ob_{\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2}}
restricts as an injective homomorphism to the third summand. We get

(8.4.3)

\ker(ob_{\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2}})=[\pi_{1}^{*}\ker(ob_{F_{1}})\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}H^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})]\oplus[\pi_{1}^{*}H^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}\ker(ob_{F_{2}})].

Let
\Phi:D^{b}(X\times X)\rightarrow D^{b}(X\times\hat{X})
be Orlov’s derived equivalence (
6.1.2
) and set

(8.4.4)

E:=\Phi(\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2}).

Lemma 8.4.1
.

(1)

The kernel of
ob_{\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2}}
is equal to the subspace of
HT^{2}(X\times X)
annihilating

ch(\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2})
.

(2)

The kernel of
ob_{E}

is equal to the subspace of
HT^{2}(X\times\hat{X})
annihilating

ch(E)
.

Proof.

(
1
)
Let
Z
be the subspace of
HT^{2}(X\times X)
annihilating
ch(\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2})
.
The inclusion
\ker(ob_{\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2}})\subset Z
follows from
[
Hua
, Theorem B]
.
Equation (
8.4.3
) implies that
\ker(ob_{\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2}})
is contained in

(8.4.5)

[\pi_{1}^{*}HT^{2}(X)\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}HT^{0}(X)]\oplus[\pi_{1}^{*}HT^{0}(X)\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}HT^{2}(X)].

We have seen that the kernel of
ob_{F_{i}}
is equal to the subspace of
HT^{2}(X)
annihilating
ch(F_{i})
under the action of
HT^{*}(X)
on its module
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
(Corollary
8.3.12
).
In order to prove the inclusion
Z\subset\ker(ob_{\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2}})

it suffices to prove that
Z
is contained in (
8.4.5
), by the decomposition (
8.4.1
) of the Atiyah class of
\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2}
.
The homomorphism

(\pi_{1}^{*}ch(F_{1})\cup\pi_{2}^{*}ch(F_{2}))\rfloor(\bullet):HT^{2}(X\times X)\rightarrow H^{*}(X\times X,{\mathbb{C}})

maps the third summand
\pi_{1}^{*}HT^{1}(X)\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}HT^{1}(X)
in the decomposition (
8.4.2
) to a subspace of
H^{*}(X\times X,{\mathbb{C}})
intersecting trivially the sum of the images of the other two summands.
Indeed, the first summand in (
8.4.2
) is mapped into
\pi_{1}^{*}H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}ch(F_{2})
,
the second into
\pi_{1}^{*}ch(F_{1})\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
and every element in the direct sum of the latter two
is the sum of classes of the form
\pi_{1}^{*}\alpha\cup\pi_{2}^{*}\beta
, where either
\alpha
or
\beta
is a Hodge class.
On the other hand, the image of
\pi_{1}^{*}HT^{1}(X)\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}HT^{1}(X)
is contained in the subspace

\pi_{1}^{*}[\oplus_{q-p=1}H^{p,q}(X)]\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}[\oplus_{q-p=1}H^{p,q}(X)].

In order to prove that
Z
is contained in (
8.4.5
) it suffices to prove that the homomorphism

ch(F_{i})\rfloor(\bullet):HT^{1}(X)\rightarrow H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
is injective.
Indeed, multiplication by
1={\rm rank}(F_{i})
induces an injective homomorphism from the subspace
H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
of
HT^{1}(X)
to the subspace
H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
of
H^{1}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
and contraction with
ch_{2}(F_{i})=-\frac{n}{2}\Theta^{2}
induces an injective homomorphism from
H^{0}(TX)
to
H^{1,2}(X)
.

(
2
)
Apply Lemma
8.3.10

with
A=X\times X
,
B=X\times\hat{X}
, and
F=\pi_{1}^{*}F_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}F_{2}
.
∎

8.5.
Orlov’s isomorphism
\Phi^{HT}:HT^{2}(X\times X)\rightarrow HT^{2}(X\times\hat{X})
maps diagonal deformations to commutative-gergy ones

The algebra
HT^{*}(X)
acts on its module
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}}):=\oplus H^{p,q}(X)
and embeds in
{\rm End}(H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))
.
Given
\alpha\in HT^{*}(X)
denote by
e_{\alpha}\in{\rm End}(H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))
the corresponding endomorphism.
Let
\tau
be the involution in (
1.2.3
).
If
\alpha
is an element of
H^{i}(\wedge^{j}TX)
and
x
is a class in
H^{k}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
, then
e_{\alpha}(x)
belongs to
H^{k+i-j}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
. Set
t:=i-j
. We have

(\tau\circ e_{\alpha}\circ\tau)(x)=(-1)^{\frac{(k+t)(k+t-1)}{2}}(-1)^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}e_{\alpha}(x)=(-1)^{kt+\frac{t(t-1)}{2}}e_{\alpha}(x).

In particular, for
k
even, we have
(\tau\circ e_{\alpha}\circ\tau)(x)=(-1)^{\frac{t(t-1)}{2}}e_{\alpha}(x)
. In particular,

(\tau\circ e_{\alpha}\circ\tau)(ch(F))=(-1)^{\frac{t(t-1)}{2}}e_{\alpha}(ch(F))

Let
(\bullet)^{*}:HT^{*}(X)\rightarrow HT^{*}(X)
act on
H^{i}(\wedge^{j}TX)
by multiplication by
(-1)^{\frac{(i-j)(i-j-1)}{2}}
.
We get

(8.5.1)

(\tau\circ e_{\alpha}\circ\tau)(ch(F))=e_{\alpha^{*}}(ch(F)).

Recall that
\tau(ch(F))=ch(F^{\vee})
.
In particular,
e_{\alpha^{*}}
annihilates
ch(F^{\vee})
, if and only if
e_{\alpha}
annihilates
ch(F)
.
For
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in HT^{2}(X)=H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\oplus H^{1}(TX)\oplus H^{0}(\wedge^{2}TX)
we have

(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)^{*}=(-\alpha,\beta,-\gamma).

The
diagonal embedding
of
HT^{2}(X)
in
HT^{2}(X\times X)
is given by
\alpha\mapsto\pi_{1}^{*}(\alpha)+\pi_{2}^{*}(\alpha)
.
We let the involution
id\otimes(\bullet)^{*}
act on
HT^{*}(X\times X)
via the Künneth decomposition of the latter.

Given an equivalence
F:D^{b}(X)\rightarrow D^{b}(Y)
of the derived categories of two smooth projective varieties
X
and
Y
we get the graded ring isomorphism

F^{HT}:HT^{*}(X)\rightarrow HT^{*}(Y)
(see
[
Ca2
, Cor. 8.3]
and
[
CBR
, Theorem 1.4]
). The summand
HT^{2}(X)
parametrizes first order deformations of
D^{b}(X)
associated to first order deformations of the abelian category of coherent sheaves on
X

[
T
]
.
The summand
HT^{1}(X)
is the Lie algebra of the identity component of
{\rm Aut}(D^{b}(X))
, and
F^{HT}
restricts to the differential of the isomorphism induced by conjugation by
F
. If
F=f_{*}
, for an isomorphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
, then
f_{*}^{HT}
restricts to the summands
H^{1}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
and
H^{0}(X,TX)

of
HT^{1}(X)
as the homomorphism induced by the direct image functor composed with the isomorphism induced by the natural sheaf isomorphisms
f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}
and
df:f_{*}TX\rightarrow TY
. When
X
is an abelian variety, the equivalence
\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}:D^{b}(\hat{X})\rightarrow D^{b}(X)
with Fourier-Mukai kernel the Poincaré line bundle
{\mathcal{P}}
conjugates autoequivalences associated to translation automorphisms to autoequivalences associated with tensorization by line bundles in
{\rm Pic}^{0}
. Hence,
\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}^{HT}:HT^{1}(\hat{X})\rightarrow HT^{1}(X)
maps the Lie subalgebra
H^{0}(T\hat{X})
of the subgroup
\hat{X}
of translations of
\hat{X}
to the Lie subalgebra
H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
of the subgroup
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
and it maps
H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{X}})
to
H^{0}(TX)
.

Lemma 8.5.1
.

The composition

\Phi^{HT}\circ(id\otimes(\bullet)^{*}):HT^{2}(X\times X)\rightarrow HT^{2}(X\times\hat{X})

maps the diagonal embedding of
HT^{2}(X)
into
H^{1}(T[X\times\hat{X}])\oplus H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X\times\hat{X}})
.
The image is the direct sum of the graphs of the following three homomorphisms:

(1)

The graph in
\pi_{1}^{*}H^{1}(TX)\oplus\pi_{2}^{*}H^{1}(T\hat{X})
of the isomorphism

\Psi_{{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}[n]}^{HT}:H^{1}(TX)\rightarrow H^{1}(T\hat{X})
.

(2)

The graph in
\pi_{1}^{*}H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\oplus H^{1}(T[X\times\hat{X}])

of the homomorphism

\displaystyle H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle H^{1}(T[X\times\hat{X}])

\displaystyle\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{*}(\eta_{1})\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}(\Psi_{{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}[n]}^{HT}(\eta_{2}))-\pi_{1}^{*}(\eta_{2})\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}(\Psi_{{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}[n]}^{HT}(\eta_{1})),

where
\eta_{i}\in H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
,
i=1,2
.

(3)

The graph in
\pi_{2}^{*}H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{X}})\oplus H^{1}(T[X\times\hat{X}])

of the homomorphism

\displaystyle H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{X}})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle H^{1}(T[X\times\hat{X}])

\displaystyle\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle-\pi_{1}^{*}(\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}^{HT}(\eta_{1}))\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}(\eta_{2})+\pi_{1}^{*}(\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}^{HT}(\eta_{2}))\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}(\eta_{1}),

where
\eta_{i}\in H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{X}})
,
i=1,2
.

In particular, the image of
HT^{2}(X)
in
HT^{2}(X\times\hat{X})
projects injectively into the direct summand
H^{1}(T[X\times\hat{X}])
.

Proof.

\Phi^{HT}:HT^{*}(X\times X)\rightarrow HT^{*}(X\times\hat{X})
is a graded ring isomorphism.

HT^{*}(X\times X)
is generated by
HT^{1}(X\times X)
.
The isomorphism
\Phi^{HT}_{{\mathcal{P}}}:D^{b}(\hat{X})\rightarrow D^{b}(X)
, associated to the Poincaré line bundle, maps

H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{X}})
to
H^{0}(TX)
and
H^{0}(T\hat{X})
to
H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
. Hence, its inverse
\Psi^{HT}_{{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}[n]}

maps
H^{0}(TX)
to
H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{X}})
and
H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
to
H^{0}(T\hat{X})
.

The isomorphism
(\mu^{-1})_{*}=\mu^{*}:D^{b}(X\times X)\rightarrow D^{b}(X\times\hat{X})
induces the isomorphism

(\mu^{-1})_{*}^{HT}:HT^{1}(X\times X)\rightarrow HT^{1}(X\times X),

where
\mu^{-1}(x,y)=(x-y,y)
.
Given
\xi_{2},\xi_{2}\in H^{0}(TX)
, we have

\displaystyle(\mu^{-1}_{*})^{HT}(\pi_{1}^{*}(\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{*}(\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2}).

\displaystyle(\mu^{-1}_{*})^{HT}(\pi_{2}^{*}(\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[-\pi_{1}^{*}\xi_{1}+\pi_{2}^{*}\xi_{1}]\wedge[-\pi_{1}^{*}\xi_{2}+\pi_{2}^{*}\xi_{2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{*}(\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2})-\pi_{1}^{*}\xi_{1}\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\xi_{2}+\pi_{1}^{*}\xi_{2}\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\xi_{1}+\pi_{2}^{*}(\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2}).

On the other hand, given
\eta\in H^{1}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
,

\displaystyle\mu^{*}(\pi_{1}^{*}(\eta))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\pi_{1}\circ\mu)^{*}(\eta)=\pi_{1}^{*}\eta+\pi_{2}^{*}\eta,

\displaystyle\mu^{*}(\pi_{2}^{*}(\eta))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{2}^{*}\eta.

Hence, given
\xi\in H^{0}(X,TX)
and
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in H^{1}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
,

\displaystyle\mu^{*}(\pi_{1}^{*}(\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{*}(\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2})+(\pi_{1}^{*}\eta_{1}\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\eta_{2})-(\pi_{1}^{*}\eta_{2}\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\eta_{1})+\pi_{2}^{*}(\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}),

\displaystyle\mu^{*}(\pi_{1}^{*}(\xi\wedge\eta))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{*}\xi\wedge(\pi_{1}^{*}\eta+\pi_{2}^{*}\eta).

\displaystyle\mu^{*}(\pi_{2}^{*}(\xi\wedge\eta))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[-\pi_{1}^{*}\xi+\pi_{2}^{*}\xi]\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\eta=-(\pi_{1}^{*}\xi\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\eta)+\pi_{2}^{*}(\xi\wedge\eta).

Given
\xi_{i}\in H^{0}(TX)
, set
\eta_{i}:=\Psi_{{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}[n]}^{HT}(\xi_{i})\in H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{X}})
,
i=1,2
.

(\mu^{-1}_{*})^{HT}(\pi_{2}^{*}(\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2}))
is sent via
(1\boxtimes\Psi_{{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}[n]})^{HT}
to

\Phi^{HT}(\pi_{2}^{*}(\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2}))=\pi_{1}^{*}(\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2})-\pi_{1}^{*}\xi_{1}\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\eta_{2}+\pi_{1}^{*}\xi_{2}\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\eta_{1}+\pi_{2}^{*}(\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}).

On the other hand,
\Phi^{HT}(\pi_{1}^{*}(\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2}))=\pi_{1}^{*}(\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2})
. Hence,

(8.5.2)

\Phi^{HT}(-\pi_{1}^{*}(\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2})+\pi_{2}^{*}(\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2}))=-\pi_{1}^{*}\xi_{1}\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\eta_{2}+\pi_{1}^{*}\xi_{2}\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\eta_{1}+\pi_{2}^{*}(\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}).

The non-commutative first order deformation
-\pi_{1}^{*}(\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2})+\pi_{2}^{*}(\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2})
of
X\times X
in
H^{0}(\wedge^{2}T(X\times X))
is mapped to a commutative-gerby deformation of
X\times\hat{X}
.

Let
\eta^{\prime}_{i}\in H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
and
\xi^{\prime}_{i}\in H^{0}(T\hat{X})
satisfy
\Psi_{{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}[n]}^{HT}(\eta^{\prime}_{i})=\xi^{\prime}_{i}
,
i=1,2
. Then

\displaystyle\Phi^{HT}(\pi_{1}^{*}(\eta^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\eta^{\prime}_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{*}(\eta^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\eta^{\prime}_{2})+(\pi_{1}^{*}\eta^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\xi^{\prime}_{2})-(\pi_{1}^{*}\eta^{\prime}_{2}\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\xi^{\prime}_{1})+\pi_{2}^{*}(\xi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\xi^{\prime}_{2}).

\displaystyle\Phi^{HT}(\pi_{2}^{*}(\eta^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\eta^{\prime}_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{2}^{*}(\xi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\xi^{\prime}_{2}).

Hence,

(8.5.3)

\Phi^{HT}(\pi_{1}^{*}(\eta^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\eta^{\prime}_{2})-\pi_{2}^{*}(\eta^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\eta^{\prime}_{2}))=\pi_{1}^{*}(\eta^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\eta^{\prime}_{2})+(\pi_{1}^{*}\eta^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\xi^{\prime}_{2})-(\pi_{1}^{*}\eta^{\prime}_{2}\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\xi^{\prime}_{1}).

Let
\eta^{\prime}\in H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
and
\xi^{\prime}\in H^{0}(T\hat{X})
satisfy
\Psi_{{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}[n]}^{HT}(\eta^{\prime})=\xi^{\prime}
.
Let
\xi\in H^{0}(TX)
and
\eta\in H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{X}})
satisfy
\Psi_{{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}[n]}^{HT}(\xi)=\eta
.
Then

\displaystyle\Phi^{HT}(\pi_{1}^{*}(\xi\wedge\eta^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{*}(\xi\wedge\eta^{\prime})+(\pi_{1}^{*}\xi\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\xi^{\prime})).

\displaystyle\Phi^{HT}(\pi_{2}^{*}(\xi\wedge\eta^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(\pi_{1}^{*}\xi\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}\xi^{\prime})+\pi_{2}^{*}(\eta\wedge\xi^{\prime}).

\displaystyle\Phi^{HT}(\pi_{1}^{*}(\xi\wedge\eta^{\prime}+\pi_{2}^{*}(\xi\wedge\eta^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{*}(\xi\wedge\eta^{\prime})+\pi_{2}^{*}(\eta\wedge\xi^{\prime}).

So
\Phi^{HT}
maps the diagonal commutative first order deformations of
X\times X
to commutative first order deformations of
X\times\hat{X}
. Furthermore, it maps the anti-diagonal non-commutative and gerby deformations of
X\times X
to
commutative and gerby deformations of
X\times\hat{X}
, by Equations
(
8.5.2
) and
(
8.5.3
).
∎

Let
F_{1}
,
F_{2}
, and
E
be as in Equation (
8.4.4
). Note that
ch(F_{1})=ch(F_{2})
. Hence,
\ker(ob_{F_{1}})=\ker(ob_{F_{2}})
, by Corollary
8.3.12
.

Corollary 8.5.2
.

The isomorphism
\Phi^{HT}\circ(id\otimes(\bullet)^{*}):HT^{2}(X\times X)\rightarrow HT^{2}(X\times\hat{X})
maps the diagonal embedding of the
9
-dimensional subspace
\ker(ob_{F_{1}})
of
HT^{2}(X)
to
a
9
-dimensional subspace of
H^{1}(T[X\times\hat{X}])\oplus H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X\times\hat{X}})
of commutative and gerby deformations in the kernel of
ob_{E}
.

Proof.

Step 1:

We claim that
\ker(ob_{F_{i}})=\ker(ob_{F_{i}^{\vee}})
. The kernel of
of_{F_{i}}
is the annihilator of
ch(F_{i})
, which is the kernel of the following homomorphism.

\begin{array}[]{c}H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\\
\oplus\\
H^{1}(TX)\\
\oplus\\
H^{0}(\wedge^{2}TX)\end{array}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&\Theta&-(d/2)\Theta^{2}\\
\Theta&-(d/2)\Theta^{2}&-(d/6)\Theta^{3}\end{array}\right)}}{{\longrightarrow}}\begin{array}[]{c}H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\\
\oplus\\
H^{3}(\Omega^{1}_{X})\end{array}

The subspace annihilating
ch(F_{i}^{\vee})
is the kernel of the homomorphism obtained by replacing the above matrix by

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-1&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&\Theta&-(d/2)\Theta^{2}\\
\Theta&-(d/2)\Theta^{2}&-(d/6)\Theta^{3}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&-1\end{array}\right)

Hence, it suffices to show that the kernel is the direct sum of the subspace of
H^{1}(TX)
annihilating
\Theta
and the subspace of
H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\oplus H^{0}(\wedge^{2}TX)
in the kernel of
(1,-\frac{d}{2}\Theta^{2})
. Indeed, both are
9
dimensional, and so it suffices to prove the inclusion of this direct sum in the subspace annihilating
ch(F_{i})
. This follows from the fact that (i) the subspace of
H^{1}(TX)
annihilating
\Theta
is equal to the subspace annihilating
\Theta^{2}
, and (ii) the subspace of
H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\oplus H^{0}(\wedge^{2}TX)

in the kernel of
(1,-\frac{d}{2}\Theta^{2})
is equal to the subspace in the kernel of
(\Theta,-\frac{d}{6}\Theta^{3})
. Fact (i) is easy to verify. Fact (ii)
follows from the identity
\xi\rfloor c\Theta^{n}=(-1)^{i}nc(\xi\rfloor\Theta)\Theta^{n-1}
, for
\xi\in H^{0}(TX)
and
c\in H^{i}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
,
i\geq 0
, and the observation that
c(\xi\rfloor\Theta)
belongs to
H^{i+1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
. Indeed, the identity implies that the homomorphism
(\Theta,-\frac{d}{6}\Theta^{3})
is
the composition

\begin{array}[]{c}H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\\
\oplus\\
H^{0}(\wedge^{2}TX)\end{array}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&-(d/2)\Theta^{2}\end{array}\right)}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Theta\cup}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{3}(\Omega^{1}_{X})

and cup product with
\Theta
is an injective homomorphism.

Step 2:

If
e_{\alpha}
annihilates
ch(F_{1})
, then it annihilates
ch(F_{2})
and also
ch(F_{2}^{\vee})
, by Step 1, and so

e_{\alpha^{*}}
annihilates
ch(F_{2}).

Hence,
e_{\pi_{1}^{*}(\alpha)+\pi_{2}^{*}(\alpha^{*})}
annihilates

ch(F_{1}\boxtimes F_{2})
. It follows that
e_{\Phi^{HT}(\pi_{1}^{*}(\alpha)+\pi_{2}^{*}(\alpha^{*}))}
annihilates
ch(E)
, by
[
CBR
, Theorem 1.4]
.
The statement thus follows from Lemmas
8.5.1

and
8.4.1
(
2
)
∎

Remark 8.5.3
.

The image of the diagonal embedding in
HT^{2}(X\times X)
, of the kernel in
HT^{2}(X)
of
ob_{F_{1}}
, is mapped via
\Phi^{HT}\circ(id\otimes(\bullet)^{*})
into a subspace of
H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X\times\hat{X}})\oplus H^{1}(T(X\times\hat{X}))
, which projects onto the tangent space in
H^{1}(T(X\times\hat{X}))
of the moduli space of abelian varieties of Weil type. This will follow from Lemma
9.3.9
, which shows that contraction with
\exp(-c_{1}(E)/{\rm rank}(E))
induces an automorphism of
HT^{2}(X\times\hat{X})
mapping the kernel of
ob_{E}
into the subspace of
H^{1}(T(X\times\hat{X}))
tangent to the moduli space of abelian varieties of Weil type. The above automorphism is unipotent, leaving each of
H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X\times\hat{X}})\oplus H^{1}(T(X\times\hat{X}))
and
H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X\times\hat{X}})
invariant and inducing the identity on the graded summand. Hence, the automorphism restricts to the kernel of
ob_{E}
as the projection to
H^{1}(T(X\times\hat{X}))
.

Remark 8.5.4
.

All the results of Section
8
remain valid after interchanging the sheaves
F_{1}:={\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{d+1}C_{i}}(\Theta)
and
F_{2}:={\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{d+1}\Sigma_{i}}(\Theta).
The two are interchanged by the involution
\iota:X\rightarrow X
sending
L\in X={\rm Pic}^{2}(C)
to
\omega_{C}\otimes L^{-1}
. The cohomological action of
\iota
corresponds to the element in the center of
{\rm Spin}(V)
acting as the isentity on
S^{+}
and by multiplication by
-1
on
V
and
S^{-}
. It acts on
HT^{ev}(X)
, and so on
HT^{2}(X)
, as the identity and on
HT^{odd}(X)
by multiplication by
-1
.

Remark 8.5.5
.

Note that when
n=2
and
F
is a simple sheaf on an abelian surface
X
, then the kernel of
ob_{F}
is
5
-dimensional and Orlov’s derived equivalence maps it to a subspace of
H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X\times\hat{X}})\oplus H^{1}(T(X\times\hat{X}))
, which projects onto the tangent space in
H^{1}(T(X\times\hat{X}))
of the moduli space of intermediate jacobians associated to the
3
-rd cohomology of generalized kummers
[
M2
]
.

9.
A reflexive sheaf over
X\times\hat{X}
with
{\rm Spin}(V)_{P}
-invariant characteristic classes

Let
C
be a non-hyperelliptic curve of genus
3
. Let
C_{\ell_{i}}=AJ(C)+\ell_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq d+1
, be the translate in
X={\rm Pic}^{2}(C)
of the Abel-Jacobi image
AJ(C)\subset{\rm Pic}^{1}(C)
of
C
by
\ell_{i}\in{\rm Pic}^{1}(C)
.
In Section
9.1
we show that
H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{d+1}C_{i}}(2\Theta)\otimes L)
vanishes for all
L\in{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
, provided the intersection
\bigcap_{1\leq i<j\leq d+1}\tau_{-\ell_{i}-\ell_{j}}(\Theta)
is empty. Furthermore, the latter condition holds for generic
C
, if the set
\{\ell_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d+1}
is an orbit under translations by elements of a cyclic subgroup of
{\rm Pic}^{0}(C)
of order
d+1
. Set
F_{1}:={\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{d+1}C_{i}}(\Theta)
and

F_{2}:={\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{d+1}\Sigma_{i}}(\Theta)
. Set
{\mathcal{G}}:=\Phi(F_{2}\boxtimes F_{1})[-3]
, where
\Phi
is Orlov’s derived equivalence.
In Section
9.2
we show that
{\mathcal{E}}:={\mathcal{G}}^{\vee}[-1]
is a reflexive sheaf over
X\times\hat{X}
of rank
8d
, for a generic choice of the curves
C_{i}
and
\Sigma_{j}
. Furthermore,
{\mathcal{E}}
is locally free away from
(d+1)^{2}
smooth surfaces in
X\times\hat{X}
.
In Section
9.3
we choose the set
\{C_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d+1}
to consist of translates of
C_{1}
by a cyclic subgroup
G_{1}
of
{\rm Pic}^{0}(C)
and choose the set
\{\Sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d+1}
similarly, for a cyclic subgroup
G_{2}
of
{\rm Pic}^{0}(C)
. We show that a tensor product of the reflexive sheaf
{\mathcal{E}}
with a suitable twisted line bundle descends to a semiregular reflexive twisted sheaf
{\mathcal{B}}
over a quotient
Y
of
X\times\hat{X}
by a group
\bar{G}
of translations, where
\bar{G}\cong G_{1}\times G_{2}
. We then prove Theorem
1.5.1
, stating the algebraicity of the Hodge-Weil classes over abelian sixfolds of Weil type of discriminant
-1
, using the version of the Semiregularity Theorem for twisted sheaves proved in Section
7.4.2
.

9.1.
A general position assumption

Keep the notation of Section
8
.
Let
\Sigma_{t}\subset X
be the image of
C_{t}
under the involution
\iota
of
X={\rm Pic}^{2}(C)
sending
a line bundle
L
to
\omega_{C}\otimes L^{-1}
. Then
\Sigma_{t}
and
C_{t}
are not algebraically equivalent for a generic non-hyperelliptic
C
. Furthermore, the cycle
C_{t}-\Sigma_{t}
is non-torsion in the group of algebraic cycles modulo algebraic equivalence, by
[
Ce
]
.
We will not need these facts and assume only that
C
is non-hyperelliptic.

Choose a point
p\in C
and use it to identify
X
and
{\rm Pic}^{0}(C)
via tensorization with
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(2p)
endowing
X
with a group structure.
Set

C_{p}:=AJ(C)+p=\{{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(p+q)\ :\ q\in C\}.

Set
C_{i}=\tau_{s_{i}}(C_{p})
, for a point
s_{i}\in X
,
1\leq i\leq d+1
.
Set
\Sigma_{p}:=\iota(C_{p})
and
\Sigma_{i}=\tau_{t_{i}}(\Sigma_{p})
, for a point
t_{i}\in X
,
1\leq i\leq d+1
.
Assume that the curves
C_{i}
are pairwise disjoint and so are the
\Sigma_{i}
.

Assumption 9.1.1
.

Assume that
H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{d+1}C_{i}}(2\Theta)\otimes L)
vanishes, for all
L\in{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
.

Given a line bundle
L
of degree
k
, set
C_{L}:=\{L(p)\ :\ p\in C\}\subset{\rm Pic}^{k+1}(C).

Lemma 9.1.2
.

Assumption
9.1.1
holds for
d\geq 3
for a generic choice of
C_{i}
’s.
Given a subset
\{\ell_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d+1}
of
{\rm Pic}^{1}(C)
the assumption
holds for
\{C_{\ell_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{d+1}
, provided

(9.1.1)

\bigcap_{1\leq i<j\leq d+1}\tau_{-\ell_{i}-\ell_{j}}(\Theta)=\emptyset.

Proof.

Step 1:
Note first that for a generic choice of
\ell_{i}
’s the intersection (
9.1.1
) is empty.
It suffices to prove it for
d=3
. Now the intersection of generic
4
translates of
\Theta
is empty. So it suffices to observe that the morphism

t:{\rm Pic}^{1}(C)\rightarrow{\rm Pic}^{2}(C)
given by

t(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},\ell_{4})=(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},\ell_{1}+\ell_{3},\ell_{1}+\ell_{4},\ell_{2}+\ell_{3})

is surjective. Indeed, set
T=(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4})
and define
f:{\rm Pic}^{2}(C)^{4}\rightarrow{\rm Pic}^{2}(C)
by
f(T)=t_{1}+t_{2}-t_{4}
and
u:{\rm Pic}^{2}(C)^{4}\rightarrow{\rm Pic}^{2}(C)^{4}
by

u(T)=(f(T),2t_{1}-f(T),2t_{2}-f(T),2t_{3}-f(T)).

Then
f(t(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},\ell_{4}))=2\ell_{1}

and
u(t(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},\ell_{4}))=(2\ell_{1},2\ell_{2},2\ell_{3},2\ell_{4})
. It follows that
u\circ t
is surjective, and hence so is
t
, as the image of
t
must be
4
dimensional.

Step 2:
We show next that a divisor
D
in
{\mid}2\Theta{\mid}
contains the translate
C_{L}
,
L\in{\rm Pic}^{1}(C)
, if and only if
D
is the divisor
D_{E}
associated to some semistable rank
2
vector bundle
E
on
C
with trivial determinant and
L^{-1}
is a subsheaf of
E
.
Recall that for a semistable vector bundle
E
on
C
with trivial determinant the set

\{M\in{\rm Pic}^{2}(C)\ :\ H^{0}(C,E\otimes M)\neq 0\}

is the set theoretic support of a divisor
D_{E}
in
{\mid}2\Theta{\mid}
, which depends only on the
S
-equivalence class of
E

(see
[
NR
]
).
The inclusion
C_{L}\subset D_{E}
is clear, if
L^{-1}
is a subsheaf of
E
. Conversely, assume that
C_{L}
is contained in
D
,
D\in{\mid}2\Theta{\mid}
. Then
D
belongs to the projective subspace
{\mathbb{P}}[H^{0}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{L}}(2\Theta))]
of
{\mid}2\Theta{\mid}
. Now
{\mathbb{P}}[H^{0}({\mathcal{I}}_{C_{L}}(2\Theta))]
is
3
-dimensional (we postpone the proof to Lemma
9.2.8
). So it suffices to prove the claim that the subset of the moduli space of
S
-equivalence classes of semistable vector bundles of rank
2
and trivial determinant, which has a representative containing
L^{-1}
as a subsheaf, is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
. Indeed, every such equivalence class
[E]
is represented by an extension class in

{\mathbb{P}}{\rm Ext}^{1}(L,L^{-1})\cong{\mathbb{P}}H^{0}(L^{2}\otimes\omega_{C})^{*}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
. This is clear if
[E]
is represented by a vector bundle containing
L^{-1}
as a saturated subsheaf.
The semi-stable but unstable
S
-equivalence classes in
{\mathbb{P}}{\rm Ext}^{1}(L,L^{-1})
are represented by the set

L^{-1}(q)\oplus L(-q)
,
q\in C
, by
[
B
, Lemma 3.6(b)]
. This is precisely the set of
S
-equivalence classes of rank
2
semistable vector bundles of trivial determinant containing
L^{-1}
as an unsaturated subsheaf.
Finally, the map
E\mapsto D_{E}
is a linear embedding of
{\mathbb{P}}{\rm Ext}^{1}(L,L^{-1})
in
{\mid}2\Theta{\mid}
,
by
[
B
, Lemma 3.7]
, proving the claim.

Step 3:
We observe next that if
E
is a semistable vector bundle with trivial determinant on
C
,
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are two line bundles of degree
1
admitting embeddings
\iota_{k}:L_{k}^{-1}\rightarrow E
as subsheaves, and the curves
C_{L_{1}}
and
C_{L_{2}}
are disjoint in
{\rm Pic}^{2}(C)
, then the line bundle
L_{1}\otimes L_{2}
is effective. We have two cases. Case 1: If
L_{1}^{-1}
is a subbundle of
E
we get the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow L_{1}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}E\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}L_{1}\rightarrow 0

and
j_{1}\circ\iota_{2}:L_{2}^{-1}\rightarrow L_{1}
does not vanish, hence
L_{1}
is isomorphic to
L_{2}^{-1}(p+q)
, for some points
p,q\in C
.

Case 2: If
L_{1}^{-1}
is only a subsheaf, then
L_{1}^{-1}(p)
is a subbundle, for some point
p\in C
.
We get the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow L_{1}^{-1}(p)\rightarrow E\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}L_{1}(-p)\rightarrow 0.

If
L_{2}^{-1}
is a subsheaf of
L_{1}^{-1}(p)
, then
L_{2}
is isomorphic to
L_{1}(q-p)
, for some point
q\in C
, and the curves
C_{L_{1}}
and
C_{L_{2}}
both contain
L_{1}(q)\cong L_{2}(p)
and so are not disjoint. Hence,
j_{1}\circ\iota_{2}
does not vanish and
L_{2}^{-1}
is isomorphic to
L_{1}(-p-q)
, for some point
q\in C
.

Step 4:
Assume that the intersection (
9.1.1
) is empty. Then there does not exist a point
t\in{\rm Pic}^{0}(C)
, such that
2t+\ell_{i}+\ell_{j}\in\Theta
, for all
1\leq i<j\leq d+1
. Thus there does not exist a semi-stable vector bundle
E
of trivial determinant, such that
D_{E}
contains the union
\bigcup_{i=1}^{d+1}C_{\ell_{i}+t}
, for some
t\in{\rm Pic}^{0}(C)
, by Step 3. Hence,
H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{d+1}C_{\ell_{i}+t}}(2\Theta))

vanishes, for all
t\in{\rm Pic}^{0}(C)
, by Step 2. It follows that
H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{d+1}C_{\ell_{i}}}(\tau_{t}^{*}(2\Theta)))
vanishes, for all
t\in{\rm Pic}^{0}(C)
. Finally, the morphism
{\rm Pic}^{0}(C)\rightarrow{\rm Pic}(X)
, given by
t\mapsto\tau_{t}^{*}(2\Theta)
, is an isogeny onto the connected component of
2\Theta
.
∎

Remark 9.1.3
.

Consider the rank
4
vector bundle
{\mathcal{U}}
over
{\rm Pic}^{1}(C)
with fiber
{\mathcal{U}}_{L}:=H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{I}}_{C_{L}}(2\Theta))
over
L\in{\rm Pic}^{1}(C)
. We have seen in the proof above that
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{U}}_{L})
is naturally isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}H^{0}(C,L^{2}\otimes\omega_{C})
. Hence, one gets a morphism
\epsilon:{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{U}})\rightarrow{\mid}2\Theta{\mid}
, by

[
B
, Lemma 3.7]
. The morphism is generically finite of degree
8
onto its image, which is the Coble quartic and is isomorphic to the moduli space of equivalence classes of semistable rank
2
vector bundles of trivial determinant over
C
, by
[
NR
]
and
[
Pa
, Sec. 4.1]
. The Zariski closed subset
\epsilon^{-1}(\epsilon({\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{U}}_{L})))
is thus reducible of dimension
\geq 3
. Let
\pi:{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{U}})\rightarrow{\rm Pic}^{1}(C)
be the natural projection. The proof above shows that the components of
\epsilon^{-1}(\epsilon({\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{U}}_{L})))
other that
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{U}}_{L})
all lie in
\pi^{-1}(\tau_{L^{-1}}(\Theta))
and so do not surject onto
{\rm Pic}^{1}(C)
. Pauly shows that the generic rank
2
stable vector bundle
E
of trivial determinant contains the inverses of
8
distinct line bundles
L_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq 8
, in
{\rm Pic}^{1}(C)
and
\otimes_{i=1}^{8}L_{i}\cong\omega_{C}^{2}

[
Pa
, Lemma 4.2]
. The divisor
D_{E}
, for such
E
, contains precisely
8
translates
C_{L_{i}}
,
1\leq i\leq 8
, of
C
.

Lemma 9.1.4
.

Let
G
be a cyclic subgroup of
{\rm Pic}^{0}(C)
of order
d+1
,
d\geq 3
.
The emptiness condition (

) holds and the curves

are pairwise disjoint
for every

in

.

Proof.

The emptiness condition (

The latter emptiness condition is open in the moduli space of pairs
(C,G)
and so it suffices to verify it for a boundary pair, where

is a chain

of three elliptic curves

of

in

.
Assume that
E_{2}
intersects

at the point

of

and
E_{2}

and the point

of

, and

. We will explain below that

\Theta=\cup_{i=1}^{3}D_{i}
, where

(9.1.2)

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}\}\times{\rm Pic}^{2}(E_{2})\times{\rm Pic}^{0}(E_{3}),

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\rm Pic}^{0}(E_{1})\times\{{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{2}}(p_{0}+p_{1})\}\times{\rm Pic}^{0}(E_{3}),

\displaystyle D_{3}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\rm Pic}^{0}(E_{1})\times{\rm Pic}^{2}(E_{2})\times\{{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{3}}\}.

Choose a generator
g
of
G
.

\bigcap_{k=1}^{d+1}\tau_{kg}(\Theta)=\bigcup_{(i_{0},\dots,i_{d})\in\{1,2,3\}^{d+1}}\bigcap_{k=1}^{d+1}\tau_{kg}(D_{i_{k}}).

Each intersection

for some

We show next that the
G
-orbit of the Abel-Jacobi image of

consists of pairwise distinct curves.
Consider the following embedded copy

of

in

\displaystyle\left[{\rm Pic}^{0}(E_{1})\times\{{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{2}}(p_{0})\}\times\{{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{3}}\}\right]

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle\left[\{{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}\}\times{\rm Pic}^{1}(E_{2})\times\{{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{3}}\}\right]

\displaystyle\cup

Denote the

.
Then

,

are disjoint. The embedding

is given by

(9.1.3)

p\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}({\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}}(p-p_{0}),{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{2}}(p_{0}),{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{3}})&\mbox{if}&p\in E_{1},\\
({\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}},{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{2}}(p),{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{3}})&\mbox{if}&p\in E_{2},\\
({\mathcal{O}}_{E_{1}},{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{2}}(p_{1}),{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{3}}(p-p_{1}))&\mbox{if}&p\in E_{3}.\end{array}\right.

For

,
C^{\prime}_{i}\cap\tau_{kg}(C^{\prime}_{i})=\emptyset
, for all

, and

. Hence, the translates

,

We prove next that
C^{\prime}
is the limit of Abel-Jacobi images of smooth genus
3
curves in a flat family that degenerates to
C
.
Observe that the curve
C^{\prime}
is precisely the Brill-Noether locus of
L\in{\rm Pic}^{(0,1,0)}(C)
with
h^{0}(L)\neq 0
. Indeed, if
L=(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3})
and
s
is a non-zero global section of

, then
s
is not identically zero on
E_{2}
. The line bundle
L_{2}
is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{2}}(p)
, for a unique point
p\in E_{2}
. If

, then

does not vanish at

and

and so

and

must both be trivial, so that

is in

. If

, then

vanishes at
p_{0}
and so it must be identically zero on

, but non-zero on
E_{3}
. Hence,
L_{1}
is arbitrary, but
L_{3}
is trivial, and so
L
is in
C^{\prime}_{1}
. Similarly, if
p=p_{1}
, then
L
is in
C^{\prime}_{3}
. Choose a family
\pi:{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow S
over a smooth one-dimensional analytic base
S
with special fiber
C
over
0\in S

and generic fiber a smooth genus
3
curve. Let

be a section with
q(0)
a point of
E_{2}\setminus\{p_{0},p_{1}\}
. The section
q
determines a section

Q:S\rightarrow{\rm Pic}({\mathcal{C}}/S)
with value
{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{s}}(q(s))
over
s\in S
, hence a family of abelian varieties
\Pi:{\mathcal{J}}\rightarrow S
with connected fibers, whose generic fiber is
{\rm Pic}^{1}(C_{s})
and its
special fiber is
{\rm Pic}^{(0,1,0)}(C)
. Over
{\mathcal{C}}\times_{S}{\rm Pic}^{0}({\mathcal{C}}/S)
we have the relative (normalized) Poincaré line bundle
{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
.
Let
\tau_{Q}:{\rm Pic}^{0}({\mathcal{C}}/S)\rightarrow{\mathcal{J}}
be the isomorphism of translation by
Q
.
Let
f_{1}:{\mathcal{C}}\times_{S}{\mathcal{J}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{J}}
and
f_{2}:{\mathcal{C}}\times_{S}{\mathcal{J}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}
be the projections.
Translating
{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
by the section

and
tensoring by the pullback of the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}(q(S))
over
{\mathcal{C}}
and we get

{\mathcal{P}}:=(id\times\tau_{Q})_{*}({\mathcal{P}}_{0})\otimes f_{2}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}(q(S))

over

. The coherent torsion sheaf
R^{1}f_{*}(\omega_{f_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{P}}^{-1})
is supported as a line bundle on a relative curve
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
over

, by Cohomology and Base Change. The generic fiber of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
is the Abel-Jacobi image of
C_{s}
and the special fiber is
C^{\prime}
.

Finally observe that
\Theta
is the image of
{\rm Sym}^{2}(C^{\prime})
via the addition morphism

(the symmetric square of each irreducible component of

contracts to a curve in

).
∎

9.2.
A reflexive secant
⊠2
-sheaf over

We continue to assume Assumption

.
Set

F_{1}:={\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{d+1}C_{i}}(\Theta)\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ F_{2}:={\mathcal{I}}_{\cup_{i=1}^{d+1}\Sigma_{i}}(\Theta).

Assumption 9.2.1

(1)

(2)

22

22

Proposition 9.2.2
.

(1)

Remark 9.2.3
.

Note that the object
